#2813. Steph Curry’s defense of Ayesha exposes the ugly double standards of the sports manosphere

If Steph Curry is leading with his full government name, know that he means business. In a 90-second video released on Wednesday, from the Instagram account of "Wardell Curry", he put the internet trolls who have been attacking his wife for years on notice. "Usually the noise doesn't bother me, doesn't bother Ayesha," the four-time NBA champion and Paris Olympic knockout artist said. "But when you're trying to step on the accomplishments of my wife, and feel the need to have to jump in and give an opinion on something that you really don't know about, [it] kind of speaks to the bigger conversation on how you view women in this world and what they're allowed and what they're not allowed to do."

Posterity will remember this as the NBA's Oscar slap moment, but without the unnecessary violence. A bizarre turn in the parasocial compact when the restless fanboys pressured a superstar into publicly defending his wife. On this occasion, Curry had to defend his wife for the heinous crime of being the leading lady in a Christmas romcom: a Hallmark knockoff called Second Chance Holiday in which Ayesha Curry plays a dating expert with bad holiday luck and shares a kiss with a man who is not Wardell Stephen Curry II – her husband of 15 years and father to their four children. That apparently was a bridge too far for some of Curry's former NBA peers.

Related: Yaxel Lendeborg is the NBA's most entertaining rookie – and he's not holding back

Steph Curry defends his wife, Ayesha, against criticism of her on-screen kiss in a video posted to Instagram on Wednesday.

Sports gossips have been making a game of picking apart Steph and Ayesha's relationship since he shot to stardom at Davidson College. They seem to forget that while Steph was stealing hearts as the NCAA Tournament's baby-faced bracket buster, Ayesha was carving out a solid career in Los Angeles as a working actor, with guest spots on Disney's Hannah Montana and on CBS's Gary Unmarried opposite comedian Jay Mohr – who is now the other half of LA Lakers president Jeanie Buss, incidentally.

The critics blanched when Ayesha joined the 2010s culinary craze with a blog and a YouTube channel, in which she shared dangerous, subversive recipes such as "Stephen's five-ingredient game-day pasta". When the hustle mushroomed into a full-blown lifestyle brand – with signature kitchenware and cookbooks, a Food Network show and a landmark CoverGirl deal – the gossips filleted Ayesha for daring to upstage her more famous husband, the real Chef Curry.

No matter what Ayesha did, it seemed, there was no winning over sports fans steeped in the manosphere's retrograde views of women. They didn't approve of her sharing bikini photos online or her body language in snaps with Drake or her candor about the struggle to hold on to her sense of identity and autonomy after marriage and motherhood. The point was driven home yet again when ex-NFL players Emmanuel Acho and LeSean McCoy, perhaps misunderstanding the concept of acting, debated the Curry kiss on their macho podcast – with Acho suggesting that Ayesha, through her words and actions, keeps telegraphing to the world that "she's not that into Steph Curry".

Intimacy work has become a wedge issue in Hollywood, with a number of prominent actors saying they will no longer participate in kissing or sex scenes – forcing some directors to get creative. But the Curry speculators aren't arguing about the mechanics of making movies – a point Steph himself hastened to rebut in his video statement. They say this stuff as if they know the Currys. They don't. They say this stuff as if they're also still with their teen sweethearts. They're not – and Acho especially is one to talk. They say this stuff as if audiences might also interpret Ayesha's on-screen kiss as an act of adultery. They won't. They'll just let the 78 minutes wash over them as they fold laundry or scroll through their phones.

No matter what Ayesha did, it seemed, there was no winning over fans steeped in the manosphere's views of women

These manosphere brains insist on typecasting Ayesha while giving other prominent athlete spouses plenty of runway. The recent marriage of Coco Jones, the Grammy-winning singer and star of the Fresh Prince update Bel-Air, and Donovan Mitchell, engine of the Cleveland Cavaliers, is treated as an equal pairing. The same goes for Major League ace Paul Skenes and his gymnast-influencer girlfriend, Livvy Dunne. When Travis Kelce started dating Taylor Swift, the talk was all about how he, the Kansas City Chiefs and the NFL stood to benefit. And when Kim K and her sisters take up with high-profile athletes, the manosphere brains roll their eyes, chalk it up to the game and blame any poor performances that might result on the Kardashian curse.

But when it comes to Ayesha, there is no room for self-reflection, growth or individuality. She is, and will always be, an NBA wife in the eyes of many. Her tiresome critics would much rather see her on the sideline managing the kids than see Steph gathering them up to bask in the afterglow of his biggest moments. Weirdly, the tradcasting doesn't stop with Ayesha. Steph Curry's mother has been dealing with the same gender policing since she divorced his father, a former NBA player, in 2021 and remarried last month. That all this outrage is building at a time when gender roles in sports have become a culture-war battleground shouldn't be lost on anyone.

Ultimately, though, the relentless snooping into the Curry marriage reveals a deeper insecurity about Steph. Here's a God-fearing, wholesome family man who speaks up for female empowerment and walks the talk when he's not burying clutch threes – and he's still getting the job done for the Warriors at age 38.

Curry has spent his career making doubters rue the day they said he was too small for pro ball, and that same bracing self-belief has helped him thrive away from the court as a venture capitalist, Hollywood producer and Bay Area philanthropist. Through 18 NBA seasons, he has remained free of scandal, extramarital or otherwise – no mean feat for an NBA superstar, much less a Silicon Valley giant. And that just makes the sports and celebrity hounds who are only following along for the potential of a Will Smith – or Tiger Woods – like train wreck push harder in their relentless pursuit of dirt.

But for dedicated knuckle-draggers like Arenas and his cohort, who still don't see an alpha male when they behold Curry, it isn't just that he could be with pretty much anyone else, given his wealth and fame. It's that he chooses Ayesha and that there still isn't a rift between them to be found, even after all these years of trying.

The armchair analysis of the Currys' marriage isn't likely to stop anytime soon. But it's a stargazing exercise in the end, with vast distances between the picture in view and the elusive reality. The constant churn of speculation only serves to show the willful ignorance at play, particularly when it comes to the difference between an on-camera kiss – again, Ayesha was acting – and an act of infidelity. With his forceful social media defense, Steph didn't just offer a reminder of what true manhood looks like. He delivered a message that should sound awfully familiar to Ayesha's haters – and give them pause: Keep my wife's name out your mouth.

#2810. The Lakers’ belief in Sandro Mamukelashvili is paying off

This offseason, the Los Angeles Lakers invested a sizable amount in Sandro Mamukelashvili by inking him to a four-year, $52 million deal. Through three weeks, that's been a remarkable move as Mamukelashvili's absolutely thriving with the Lakers and providing critical, versatile minutes off the bench.

The Georgian big man is averaging 10.8 points, 4.5 rebounds and 1.6 assists per game while shooting 41.2% from deep and 57.7% on 2-pointers. Los Angeles entrusts him with varying roles, whether it's spacing the floor, operating as a playmaking hub, driving closeouts or toggling between power forward and small-ball center. It's evident the organization, whether it be players or coaches, have the utmost confidence in him.

Now, the goal is to make sure Mamukelashvili himself embraces that autonomy and is unburdened by any fear of mistakes whenever he's on the floor.

"I want him to understand how much his teammates, his coaches, myself, we believe in him. The focus on the next play, I think, is big for him," head coach JJ Redick told reporters following Wednesday's win, when Mamukelashvili recorded 23 points, three rebounds, two assists and one steal. "Being able to move on after a missed shot, a defensive mistake, whatever it may be, just move on to the next play and focus on that. But I think the big thing for him is understanding that he has a lot of freedom."

Mamukelashvili said he feels the unbridled support rippling from coaches and teammates and is optimistic he'll soon echo that credence in him. But he admitted achieving it is easier said than done.

"I hope I'll start believing in myself as much as they do," he told reporters Wednesday.

Mamukelashvili's finest two games of the season may have come Wednesday in Milwaukee and Monday against the Atlanta Hawks. During those two outings, he's scored a season-high 23 points each time and averaged 5.5 rebounds, 2.5 assists and 1.0 steals. As he continues to grow more comfortable in his new environment, performances similar to those could become the norm — much to the Lakers' benefit.

"He's just getting more confident as he plays more. He's an extremely, extremely talented offensive player," Walker Kessler said Wednesday. "We all believe in every shot he shoots and every play he makes. … We believe in his offensive game and he's getting more confident and he's really, really helping us."

#2809. Why Cade Smith Could Have Won AL Reliever of the Year

Cleveland Guardians closer Cade Smith is the runner-up for the American League Reliever of the Year Award, as voted on by the Baseball Writers' Association of America (BBWAA). This is the first year for the award.

Oct 12, 2026; St. Petersburg, Florida, USA; Cleveland Guardians pitcher Cade Smith (36) reacts with catcher Patrick Bailey (16) after defeating the Tampa Bay Rays in game one of the ALCS for the 2026 MLB Playoffs at Tropicana Field. Mandatory Credit: Kim Klement Neitzel-Imagn Images

Smith finished behind Toronto Blue Jays closer Louis Varland, who won the inaugural award on Wednesday night. Although Tampa Bay Rays closer Bryan Baker finished third in the voting, he won the Mariano Rivera AL Reliever of the Year Award, which is voted on by a panel of nine retired relief pitchers. The Rivera Award has been given annually since 2014 and features legendary Hall of Famer voters such as Trevor Hoffman, Dennis Eckersley, and Billy Wagner.

Varland, who finished with the highest fWAR and bWAR among AL relievers, earned 16 first-place votes, with Smith securing seven first-place votes and Baker receiving six first-place votes. Varland accrued 146 total points, with Smith receiving 133 and Baker receiving 111. Boston Red Sox closer Aroldis Chapman finished fourth in the voting with 36 points, and Houston Astros closer Josh Hader received a single first-place vote despite missing the first two months of the season with a bicep injury.

Still, Smith didn't leave Wednesday night without some hardware. He was named to the All-MLB Second Team, along with Baker. Varland and San Diego Padres' closer Mason Miller, who won both iterations of the NL Reliever of the Year Award, were named to the First Team.

The 27-year-old right-hander helped lead the Guardians to a division title and played a pivotal role in the team advancing to the American League Championship Series.

In his third professional season, Smith was second in the American League with a 2.6 fWAR while pitching 74 innings. He produced a 1.95 ERA (2.18 FIP) and saved 41 games, which tied for the league lead with Baker. He struck out 13.01 batters per nine innings, which was second in the AL. Also, he ranked fourth in K/BB ratio, producing 4.65 strikeouts per walk allowed. Per Baseball Savant's Pitching Run Value, his +12 PRV ranked in the 80th percentile amongst all pitchers. His chase rate (33.7%) and whiff rate (34.5%) were elite.

Previous winners of the AL Reliever of the Year (the Mariano Rivera Award, not the new BBWAA honor) include Aroldis Chapman (Boston Red Sox in 2025 and New York Yankees in 2019), Emmanuel Clase (Cleveland Guardians in 2024 and 2022), Félix Bautista (Baltimore Orioles in 2023), and Liam Hendriks (Chicago White Sox in 2021 and Oakland Athletics in 2020).

Smith was originally drafted in the 16th round of the 2017 draft by the Minnesota Twins but did not sign. He re-entered the 2020 draft and was not drafted. He would sign with Cleveland as a free agent. His rise from undrafted player to All-MLB selection is rare.

Smith relied on a four-seam fastball that was effective against both left-handed and right-handed batters. His fastball yielded a .213/.274/.316 slash line and produced a 29.9% whiff rate. His splitter completely neutralized left-handed batters, with a slash line of .092/.124/.092 and a 45.3% whiff rate. His arsenal is rounded out by a sweeper that he threw 10% of the time that yielded a .133 xBA and a 35.4% whiff rate.

Smith has placed himself directly in the conversation as one of the best closers in the sport. Dating back to 2024, Smith is second in the majors in fWAR, producing 8.0 wins above replacement in 223 innings, which is also among the leaders for relief pitchers.

During the 2026 postseason, Smith anchored a stout Cleveland bullpen. He pitched 11 1/3 innings and allowed a 2.38 ERA (2.76 xFIP). He produced 11.12 K/9 and 1.59 BB/9 and was one of the most dominant relievers to pitch in the playoffs. Only Abner Uribe of the Milwaukee Brewers pitched in more postseason games this year than Smith.

Smith will look to continue anchoring a sturdy Cleveland bullpen as they aim for another postseason in 2027.

#2808. Failed Daiyan Henley trade exposes a flaw in the NFL's trade deadline

The trade deadline is the trade deadline. Except when it’s not the trade deadline.

The strange situation involving the failed trade of Chargers linebacker Daiyan Henley to the Broncos reveals a major flaw in the NFL’s deadline-day machinery.

Adam Schefter of ESPN has provided the details (which we have confirmed to be accurate) in the most nonchalant, innocuous terms possible: “It is not uncommon for one team to have some medical concerns about a player while another does not. In this case, Denver had concerns about Henley following his physical. Even then, the Broncos still wanted Henley with Denver and LA discussing revised trade alternatives throughout the day, taking into account those concerns. In the end, no agreement was reached. The deal was off.”

Here’s what it means. The Chargers and Broncos struck a deal for Henley and independently communicated the terms to the NFL before the trade deadline of 4:00 p.m. ET on Tuesday, November 10. Like all trades, the Henley deal was subject to the player passing a physical.

The Broncos discovered an issue with Henley’s knee. It has, we’re told, arthritis. (As one source with neither the Chargers nor the Broncos told PFT, most if not all starting linebackers in the NFL have some degree of arthritis in their knees.)

The NFL allows trade terms to be revised after the deadline, to an extent. Per a league spokesperson: “We would permit them to change the pick compensation. They would not be able to add a player or trade a different player.”

Instead of concluding that the physical had been failed and canceling the deal (like the Ravens did with the trade for Raiders defensive end Maxx Crosby in March), the Broncos were willing to proceed with revised terms. In other words, for something less than a fourth-round pick in 2027 and a fifth-round pick in 2028, they’d still do the deal.

That’s not how it should work after the deadline. The window has closed. The deals are done, or they’re not. After 4:00 p.m. ET on November 10, the only question left to answer is whether the player passes or fails the physical.

As one source explained it, deadline-day deals have gone through in the past with terms that were revised after the deadline.

Based on Schefter’s reporting, that’s what happened here. The Broncos had “medical concerns,” but not enough to reject the player. The Broncos and Chargers discussed “revised trade alternatives” on Wednesday — the day after the window for doing trades ended.

The Chargers could have taken less in order to get the deal done. They chose not to do so. The fact that the rules allow trade terms to be renegotiated after the deadline opened the door for the Broncos to squeeze the Chargers.

The situation creates an opportunity for shenanigans. If, for example, Team A is preparing to trade a player to Team B at the deadline for a fourth-round pick and a sixth-round pick, Team C can swoop in and offer a fourth-round pick and a fifth-round pick. Then, when the physical is performed after the deadline, Team C can say, “Well, we have medical concerns. If we don’t agree to reduce the package to a fourth-round pick and a sixth-round pick, we’re going to fail the player on his physical.”

That’s not to say the Broncos did it deliberately. Obviously, however, they remained interested in Henley (who has started all eight games this year and has participated in 89 percent of the snaps) at something less than the agreed terms.

The point is that this little-known ability to extend the trading period by giving the acquiring team the ability to use the physical to leverage better terms shouldn’t exist. When a deal is done at the deadline, the trade should either go through for the agreed terms or it shouldn’t.

It opens the door to gamesmanship. The team that has traded the player has no alternatives. It can’t trade the player to someone else. It either has to accept reduced terms or try to put the toothpaste back in the tube.

The Vikings, for example, could have tried to do that to the Raiders regarding quarterback Kirk Cousins. “Well, we found something in Kirk’s leg that we don’t like. We’re not comfortable giving up a third-round pick. How about a fifth-round pick instead?”

What would the Raiders have done at that point? They’d already turned the page to Fernando Mendoza, and they spent Tuesday and Wednesday getting him ready to play.

The rule needs to change. The ability to revisit trade terms after the deadline has passed should be eliminated. Either the deal gets done as negotiated, or it doesn’t.

Hovering over all of this is the subjectivity that can result in a team failing a player on a physical if it wants that outcome. It’s an easy way out for a team that has buyer’s remorse. (The Bengals, for example, could have determined that Eagles defensive end Arnold Ebiketie failed his physical, if the Bengals hadn’t realized until after the trade terms were finalized that the Bengals would lose a 2027 third-round compensatory pick if the trade became official.)

At some point, it makes sense for the physicals not to be conducted by team doctors but by an independent, unaffiliated physician. But that’s for another day. For now, the immediate problem that needs to be solved is the ability of teams to renegotiate trade terms after the trade deadline.

No one benefits from that, and unscrupulous teams could use it to their advantage.

#2805. Mosley baseball pitcher Ryan Harless signs with Louisville

Mosley baseball standout Ryan Harless will continue his career at the University of Louisville after signing with the Cardinals Thursday afternoon.

Harless, who has been committed to Louisville for over a year, said he was excited to make the opportunity official.

Mosley head baseball coach and athletic director Doug Lee praised Harless' impact on the Dolphins, pointing to his leadership, vand work ethic throughout his high school career.

"Every team that I've been a part of that Ryan's been a part of, he's been the ace of that staff," Lee said. "He's always led the team on the mound, had a presence, and honestly was always an offensive threat."

Harless put together a standout junior season, hitting .398 with a .500 on-base percentage, 18 RBIs and one home run. On the mound, he went 10-1 with a 0.41 ERA and 121 strikeouts in 67 2/3 innings. Harless was voted the Bay County Baseball Player of the Year last season.

He also went his first 45 innings of the season without allowing an earned run and recorded 42 consecutive scoreless innings.

Playoff football predictions Reporter, AI face off on Bay County high school football playoff picks

Local soccer The Jamaican connection leading Bay High soccer's championship aspirations

"His first 45 innings on the mound last year, he gave up zero earned runs," Lee said. "I don't know if I've ever seen a high school pitcher do that."

Harless' performance earned him the Florida Dairy Farmers 2026 Class 5A Baseball Player of the Year award.

Lee said Harless' success is a reflection of the work he puts in beyond scheduled practices.

"I think the biggest thing that I see in Ryan that I think has led him to this moment is just work ethic," Lee said. "He is constantly always doing stuff."

Harless credited his time at Mosley and the relationships he has developed with the coaching staff for helping elevate his game.

"That's kind of elevated my game because I've been playing for guys that I've loved for a long time," Harless said. "Watching them compete and playing for them has really elevated my game."

Before heading to Louisville, Harless has one more season with the Dolphins. His focus is on making the most of his senior year.

"I want to try to win as many games as I can and win a state championship before I go to college," Harless said.
